They have also added the [Force Training](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Training "Force Training") Feat, giving them the Force Power *[Farseeing](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Farseeing "Farseeing")*, as well as the **[Visions](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Visions "Visions")** Talent from the [Sense Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sense_Talent_Tree "Sense Talent Tree").

At 7th level, they complete the [Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi "Jedi") Heroic Class and enters the [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") Prestige Class, finally reaching their goal. Like the Sith Manipulator, they either have behaved in such a way to garner the appropriate number of [Dark Side Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dark_Side_Points "Dark Side Points") or will automatically get them by falling to the corruption of [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") and completing their [Destiny](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny "Destiny"). They have also added the **[Dark Scourge](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dark_Scourge "Dark Scourge")** Talent from the [Sith Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Talent_Tree "Sith Talent Tree") as well as the [Force Training](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Training "Force Training") Feat twice, which gives them the Force Powers *[Force Grip](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Grip "Force Grip")* and *[Negate Energy](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Negate_Energy "Negate Energy")* (plus any additional [Force Powers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Powers "Force Powers") based on their [Wisdom](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Wisdom "Wisdom") bonus). They also choose a new [Corruption](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Corruption "Corruption") or [Destruction](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destruction "Destruction") Destiny.

*By Matthew Grau*

Previous articles looked at the essence of [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith"), choosing an appropriate name and appearance for your Sith character, and the game mechanics of designing that character. In this article, we delve into the technology used by Sith, the kinds of characters that best fit in the different eras, and ways to advance beyond the [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") Prestige Class.

## Technology

The most notable piece of technology associated with a Sith is the [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber"). This ancient and elegant weapon is the tool of both [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi") and [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith").

To [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi"), the crafting of a [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber") is a sacred task. Apprentices are taken to the ritual caves that hold the crystals necessary to create the focused core of the Jedi's weapon. The assembly is part technical, part manipulation of [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force"). When it is done, the color of a [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber") depends on the type of crystal used in its creation. Yellow, blue, and green are common. Some instead hunt far and wide for their crystal, with results (such as Mace Windu's violet [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber")) that are the stuff of legends.

To [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith"), this is needlessly ritualistic. A [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber") is a tool like any other, and all that matters is its wielder. For this reason, [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") artificially grow the crystals for their [Lightsabers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsabers "Lightsabers"). The outcome of such is consistent and distinct- all Sith [Lightsabers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsabers "Lightsabers") are red.

The [Double-Bladed Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Double-Bladed_Lightsaber "Double-Bladed Lightsaber") is the notorious weapon wielded by [Darth Maul](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Maul "Darth Maul") during [The Rise of the Empire Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rise_of_the_Empire_Era "The Rise of the Empire Era"). These tricky weapons take time to master, but they can make one man fight as two. Some mistakenly believe that [Darth Maul](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Maul "Darth Maul") created the weapon, when in fact it was first unleashed under the fury of the Sith Lord [Exar Kun](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Exar_Kun "Exar Kun") in ancient times. Those Sith Lords who lean toward the savagery of physical violence may find themselves drawn to the artistry and deadliness of the [Double-Bladed Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Double-Bladed_Lightsaber "Double-Bladed Lightsaber").

Normally, attacking with both ends of a [Double-Bladed Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Double-Bladed_Lightsaber "Double-Bladed Lightsaber") incurs a -10 penalty to both attacks. The Dual Weapon Mastery [Feats](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Feats "Feats") will dramatically reduce that penalty. [Dual Weapon Mastery I](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dual_Weapon_Mastery_I "Dual Weapon Mastery I"), which reduces the penalty to -5, can be taken during character creation. [Dual Weapon Mastery II](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dual_Weapon_Mastery_II "Dual Weapon Mastery II") and [Dual Weapon Mastery III](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dual_Weapon_Mastery_III "Dual Weapon Mastery III") require higher [Dexterity](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dexterity "Dexterity") scores and advanced levels to acquire. Unless your character has a high [Strength](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Strength "Strength") modifier or the [Weapon Focus](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Weapon_Focus "Weapon Focus") Feat, [Dual Weapon Mastery II](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dual_Weapon_Mastery_II "Dual Weapon Mastery II") is what's needed to be truly deadly. Since this Feat requires a high [Dexterity](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dexterity "Dexterity"), the [Weapon Finesse](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Weapon_Finesse "Weapon Finesse") Feat can offset these penalties nicely. The Force Power *[Dark Rage](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dark_Rage "Dark Rage")* also helps.

The [Sith Lanvarok](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Lanvarok "Sith Lanvarok") is a wrist-mounted projectile weapon from ancient times, exceedingly rare by the days of [The Clone Wars](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Clone_Wars "The Clone Wars"). Inaccurate and with a long reload time, the [Sith Lanvarok](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Lanvarok "Sith Lanvarok") hurled a spray of metal discs to distract an enemy or as a weapon of first attack. However, Sith could use [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force") to guide the discs, making them far more dangerous than the weapon would be in the hands of a normal wielder.

[The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith"), like [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi"), travel light. As long as they have robes, a cloak (probably [All-Temperature](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/All-Temperature_Cloak "All-Temperature Cloak")), and a [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber"), they are content. Some also travel with currency and a [Utility Belt](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Utility_Belt "Utility Belt") that might contain three days' worth of food capsules, a [Medpac](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Medpac "Medpac"), a [Tool Kit](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Tool_Kit "Tool Kit"), a spare [Power Pack](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Power_Pack "Power Pack"), a spare [Energy Cell](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Energy_Cell "Energy Cell"), a [Glow Rod](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Glow_Rod "Glow Rod"), a [Liquid Cable Dispenser](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Liquid_Cable_Dispenser "Liquid Cable Dispenser") with grappling hook, an [Aquata Breather](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Aquata_Breather "Aquata Breather"), [Electrobinoculars](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Electrobinoculars "Electrobinoculars"), or a few specialized [Tools](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Tools "Tools") with which to do one's dark will. [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") are happy to rely on their wits and their exceptional power.

Certain [Starfighters](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Starfighters "Starfighters") might appeal to a Sith Lord. Those who were once Jedi may prefer a Jedi fighter to other craft. Those who served before the days of [The Clone Wars](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Clone_Wars "The Clone Wars") would pilot the [Delta-7 *Aethersprite* Interceptor](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Delta-7_Aethersprite_Interceptor "Delta-7 Aethersprite Interceptor"), whereas those who fought in [The Clone Wars](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Clone_Wars "The Clone Wars") would prefer the [Eta-2 *Actis* Interceptor](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Eta-2_Actis_Interceptor "Eta-2 Actis Interceptor"). Those in the favor of [Count Dooku](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Count_Dooku "Count Dooku") might also receive one of the rare and exotic [*Ginivex*-Class Starfighters](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Ginivex-Class_Starfighters "Ginivex-Class Starfighters"). During [The Old Republic Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Old_Republic_Era "The Old Republic Era"), [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") had their own [Starfighters](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Starfighters "Starfighters"), known as [Sith Interceptors](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Interceptors "Sith Interceptors"). Only one craft was designed for [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") in [The Rise of the Empire Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rise_of_the_Empire_Era "The Rise of the Empire Era"), [The Rebellion Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rebellion_Era "The Rebellion Era"), and [The New Jedi Order Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_New_Jedi_Order_Era "The New Jedi Order Era")- a prototype that is owned by [Darth Sidious](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Sidious "Darth Sidious") and carefully guarded. The [Sith Infiltrator](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Infiltrator "Sith Infiltrator") is truly a craft worthy of the tradition of the Sith Lords.

Planet-side, Sith Lords prefer [Speeder Bikes](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Speeder_Bikes "Speeder Bikes") for their speed and maneuverability. The reflexes of a Force-user also make the bikes far less dangerous and more effective than they might be for the average person. For similar reasons, when bikes are impractical, Sith instead might choose racing or military [Speeders](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Speeders "Speeders").

## Eras

The era in which you choose to play has great bearing on what kind of Sith your character can be.

[The Old Republic Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Old_Republic_Era "The Old Republic Era") can call for any kind of Sith because they are prevalent in the universe. There will be more Sith Warriors than any other kind; the strongest of [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") are those who rule. Characters with strong backgrounds in the [Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi "Jedi"), [Soldier](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Soldier "Soldier"), and perhaps [Scout](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Scout "Scout") Heroic Classes will be the most common, favoring [Skills](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Skills "Skills") and [Feats](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Feats "Feats") designed to enhance combat ability and [Force Powers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Powers "Force Powers").

[The Rise of the Empire Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rise_of_the_Empire_Era "The Rise of the Empire Era"), [The Rebellion Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rebellion_Era "The Rebellion Era"), and [The New Jedi Order Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_New_Jedi_Order_Era "The New Jedi Order Era") call for the Rule of Two, which requires the ways of stealth and guile. However, all Sith are trained as warriors as well, since one Sith must be a match for several Jedi. Characters with backgrounds in either the [Noble](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Noble "Noble") or [Scoundrel](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Scoundrel "Scoundrel") Heroic Classes, balanced with [Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi "Jedi") and either [Soldier](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Soldier "Soldier") or [Scout](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Scout "Scout"), are more the order of the day, favoring skills of manipulation and intimidation as well as those of combat.

[The Legacy Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Legacy_Era "The Legacy Era") begins with the Rule of Two and follows the same maxims as the previous three eras. However, once [Darth Krayt](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Krayt "Darth Krayt") takes his rightful place in the universe, [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") flood out again. Sith Warriors again are common, as they were in [The Old Republic Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Old_Republic_Era "The Old Republic Era").

## Advancement Paths

Once you have arrived at the [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") Prestige Class, your character is now true Sith. Its been a bit of a journey so far, but where do you go from here?

Unless you have chosen a diverse array of [Classes](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Classes "Classes") (either Prestige or Heroic) as your character's background, you have only a few directions in which to grow. The four [Talent Trees](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talent_Trees "Talent Trees") from which a [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") draws are the [Armor Specialist Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Armor_Specialist_Talent_Tree "Armor Specialist Talent Tree"), the [Duelist Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Duelist_Talent_Tree "Duelist Talent Tree"), the [Lightsaber Combat Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber_Combat_Talent_Tree "Lightsaber Combat Talent Tree"), and the [Sith Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Talent_Tree "Sith Talent Tree"), the only unique tree in this class. Your character's abilities will now aim toward vicious combat and growing in [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") of [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force").

If you have chosen a diversity of [Classes](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Classes "Classes") as your character's background, you'll have more room to play. You can continue to develop your character in other [Classes](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Classes "Classes") that can support their growth as a Sith, using the guidelines presented in Part 3: Design.

One unique way your character can develop is with the [Dreadful Countenance](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Dreadful_Countenance "Dreadful Countenance") Feat. It is a Feat solely devoted to the way Sith change in appearance and the effect it has on them. The requirements for the Feat are a [Charisma](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Charisma "Charisma") of 13 and membership in [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") tradition, the latter accomplished by reaching the [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") Prestige Class.

Upon becoming a member of the [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") Prestige Class, your character will have access to [Force Techniques](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Techniques "Force Techniques"), which represent a deeper understanding of [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force"). Starting at 2nd level, they gain a new [Force Technique](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Technique "Force Technique") every other level, improving and extending other mechanical uses of [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force").

As your [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") grows in levels, they gain access to the more advanced [Prestige Classes](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Prestige_Classes "Prestige Classes"). The most obvious place to go is the [Sith Lord](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Lord "Sith Lord") Prestige Class. There are only two requirements that differ from the [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice"): your character must be at least 12th level and must know at least one [Force Technique](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Technique "Force Technique"). At this point, your character is purged of fear and begins to learn the secrets of [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force"), which allow them to start using [Force Powers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Powers "Force Powers") in new and powerful ways, but, unlike [Force Techniques](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Techniques "Force Techniques"), they cost [Force Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Points "Force Points") to use. At 2nd level and every level of [Sith Lord](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Lord "Sith Lord") thereafter, your character gains one [Force Secret](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Secret "Force Secret"). Levels in the [Sith Lord](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Lord "Sith Lord") Prestige Class do not offer access to new [Talent Trees](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talent_Trees "Talent Trees"), instead continuing to develop in the [Sith Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Talent_Tree "Sith Talent Tree") and [Force Talent Trees](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Talent_Trees "Force Talent Trees"). However, your character becomes adept at using the dread tradition known as Dun Möch, which lets them tempt others to [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side").

The [Force Disciple](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Disciple "Force Disciple") is another advanced Prestige Class that might be an option for a growing Sith. This class represents a Sith who wishes to delve more deeply into the mysteries of [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side"), becoming a prophet. Your character becomes immune to [Mind-Affecting](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Mind-Affecting "Mind-Affecting") effects, gains prophetic visions, and gains access to [Force Secrets](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Secrets "Force Secrets"). Their [Talents](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talents "Talents") come from those available to a [Force Adept](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Adept "Force Adept") and from [Force Talent Trees](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Talent_Trees "Force Talent Trees"). In addition to the requirements of the [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") Prestige Class, the [Force Disciple](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Disciple "Force Disciple") Prestige Class requires that your character is 12th level and has the Force Power *[Farseeing](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Farseeing "Farseeing")*. Also, it requires at least three levels of the [Force Adept](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Adept "Force Adept") Prestige Class, since there is no other way to acquire two [Talents](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talents "Talents") from the [Talent Trees](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talent_Trees "Talent Trees") offered there.

Another interesting option, if you are willing to take the extra time, is to have your character work their way up through [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi") ranks to reach the [Jedi Master](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi_Master "Jedi Master") Prestige Class. The only requirements beyond those needed to reach [Sith Apprentice](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Apprentice "Sith Apprentice") are that your character is 12th level, has one [Force Technique](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Technique "Force Technique") (easily gained through two levels of [Jedi Knight](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi_Knight "Jedi Knight")), and is a member of [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi"). They become fearless, gains meditative focus, and learn [Force Secrets](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Secrets "Force Secrets"). The [Jedi Master](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi_Master "Jedi Master") Prestige Class confers no new [Talent Trees](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talent_Trees "Talent Trees"), instead focusing on the [Duelist Talent Tree](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Duelist_Talent_Tree "Duelist Talent Tree") and [Force Talent Trees](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Talent_Trees "Force Talent Trees"). In general, if your character follows this path to [Sith Lord](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Lord "Sith Lord"), it will take longer, but they will have access to a variety of Force-related powers and abilities to make up for it. Just imagine the power of a [Jedi Master](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi_Master "Jedi Master") who has advanced through the ranks of the [Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi "Jedi") and [Jedi Knight](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Jedi_Knight "Jedi Knight") Classes- they will be very dangerous when they finally fall to [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side").

If your character has already fulfilled their [Destiny](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny "Destiny") and taken on the mantle of Sith Master, the [Education](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Education "Education") Destiny might be in order. Perhaps they will play an important part in training a dreadful Sith who has their own [Destiny](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny "Destiny") to wreak upon the galaxy. The bonus for fulfilling this [Destiny](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny "Destiny") is a +5 bonus to one [Class Skill](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Class_Skill "Class Skill") of your choice, which is particularly useful when applied to [Use the Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Use_the_Force "Use the Force").

*By Matthew Grau*

In the last several articles, we examined how to create a Sith character from the ground up using *Saga Edition* mechanics. But what if you're a Gamemaster and you want to include Sith characters in your playing group?

Your first consideration involves how many Sith you'll have in the group. Some groups might be composed of all Sith, and others might feature only one or a few Sith while the rest are characters who compliment them. There are advantages and challenges to both.

## Group Origins

The first quandary to solve is how the character group came to be. Why are they working together? Maybe they have common goals or common enemies. How did they come together? Perhaps they had masters who were comrades, or maybe they stumbled across one another during a mission.

### All-Sith Groups

The advantage to creating All-Sith Groups is they are cut from the same cloth. They are adherents to [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") of [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force") and share solidarity of philosophy and purpose. However, the danger with All-Sith Groups is obvious- in-fighting. The strongest Sith rules, and those under them will no doubt test them. Some Sith are satisfied with their place, while others constantly strive for authority.

You could agree with your group that one of them is in charge so that dissent doesn't percolate within the ranks, though such an approach is hardly realistic given Sith nature. Another option is to have the characters be equals who are under the thumb of a significantly stronger Sith. But what happens if the alpha dog is vanquished? Try to work out such concerns in advance unless you desire a group of duplicitous characters who are at each others' throats. Whether or not you like this dynamic, you should steer your players toward such behavior, even if they don't gravitate toward it themselves. It isn't optional; it's the nature of [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith").

Perhaps the best way to deal with this problem is simply to strike an understanding among your players and build such competition into the plot. If your players intentionally create rivalries, with an agreement not to let them get in the way of broader gameplay, they can portray their Sith characters appropriately without obstructing the game or creating bad blood. In short, work it out and turn it into part of the story instead of leaving it to whims.

### Groups With One Sith

On the other hand, the advantage to creating groups with only one or a few Sith characters is that the other characters won't typically care about whether the strongest is leading. These kind of groups can create a more harmonious dynamic, but remember, [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") are selfish and serve themselves above all others. Because of their belief in strength and superiority, [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") might begin to treat the other characters as lackeys and playthings. Of course, this can prove tremendously entertaining for the Gamemaster, and even the players, if the other characters would not normally choose to be in league with a Sith. How will they react once they unravel the identity of their new friend? How will they react once they come to understand that they've struck a deal with the devil in order to succeed in a mission?

Again, the best solution to these problems is to work things out ahead of time. Depending on the type of game, some characters might not mind being lackeys of [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith")- especially if the rewards for service are significant. [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") can promise many things that appeal to many morally questionable individuals. If the rest of the characters are unaware that they've struck a bargain with a Sith, determine how that might play out before you get there. Otherwise, it is almost guaranteed to disrupt your group and the long-term goals of the game. Building this kind of plot twist into your story will create drama instead of chaos.

The type of group you create also depends upon the era in which you choose to play. In eras where [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") are plentiful (like [The Old Republic Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Old_Republic_Era "The Old Republic Era") or [The Legacy Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Legacy_Era "The Legacy Era")), All-Sith Groups are the order of the day and capable of operating openly. In eras dominated by the Rule of Two (like [The Rise of the Empire Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rise_of_the_Empire_Era "The Rise of the Empire Era"), [The Rebellion Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rebellion_Era "The Rebellion Era"), or [The New Jedi Order Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_New_Jedi_Order_Era "The New Jedi Order Era")), it makes sense to have one Sith in a group of characters who work within his or her plans. For example, in [The Rise of the Empire Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rise_of_the_Empire_Era "The Rise of the Empire Era") or [The Rebellion Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rebellion_Era "The Rebellion Era"), a group might consist of one Sith and several Dark Jedi or Dark Side [Force Adepts](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Adepts "Force Adepts").

## Adventure Hooks

Once you have your group figured out, it's time to play. In games revolving around [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith"), even more so than in other games, it's important to give the characters challenges and goals. It might be tempting to give Sith space to mess around as the dark terrors that they are, but without serious challenges and plans, they will eventually turn on each other in boredom.

In eras where the Rule of Two is predominant, you will have to create adventures in which [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") play it safe and have long-reaching goals. The other trick here is to create such adventures in a way that does not violate *Star Wars* canon. In eras where [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") dominate, you can easily create adventures in such a way that the characters float from confrontation to confrontation without worry of stealth or canon.

On that note, here are a few adventure hooks to give you guidelines. The hooks are categorized by era, but some might be able to be modified to work in other eras.

### [The Old Republic Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Old_Republic_Era "The Old Republic Era")

In an era dominated by [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith"), one kind of adventure revolves around the quest for the legacy of [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith"). This involves hunting down hidden places of Sith power long abandoned by the rest of the galaxy, chasing down legends to uncover lost secrets, and experimenting with forgotten and perhaps even forbidden power and alchemy.

As a specific example, your Sith characters might stumble across an ancient [Holocron](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Holocron "Holocron") after taking a Jedi outpost or vessel. [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi") did not open the [Holocron](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Holocron "Holocron") because doing so requires opening oneself up to [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side"); instead, they hoped to bury it in their archives forever. However, your group opens the [Holocron](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Holocron "Holocron") and discovers the secrets within, which speak of an insignificant moon named [Oricon](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Oricon?action=edit&redlink=1 "Oricon (page does not exist)") lost now in [The Outer Rim](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Outer_Rim?action=edit&redlink=1 "The Outer Rim (page does not exist)"). An ancient Sith named [Darth Vitus](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Vitus?action=edit&redlink=1 "Darth Vitus (page does not exist)") used the moon as a place to experiment with and expand his already significant knowledge of Sith Alchemy. The [Holocron](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Holocron "Holocron") provides hints on how to find [Oricon](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Oricon?action=edit&redlink=1 "Oricon (page does not exist)"), and the hunt is an adventure on its own. Once the moon is found, the characters face the descendants of Darth Vitus's experiments- horrible monsters, many of which are strong in the power of [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side"). The characters might eventually find Darth Vitus's compound, in which they could uncover powerful secrets to aid them in their war against [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi").

### [The Rise of the Empire Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rise_of_the_Empire_Era "The Rise of the Empire Era")

In an era exemplified by the Rule of Two, it will be challenging to create adventures that don't violate canon. As we know from the *Star Wars* films, [Darth Sidious](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Sidious "Darth Sidious") rules [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") in this era, with only three apprentices that we know of- first [Darth Maul](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Maul "Darth Maul"), then [Darth Tyranus](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Tyranus "Darth Tyranus"), then [Darth Vader](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Vader "Darth Vader"). However, we also know that [Darth Vader](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Vader "Darth Vader") had a secret apprentice, and it's possible for Sith groups in different parts of the galaxy to have reason to keep their existence a secret, even from other Sith.

As a specific example, your group could be composed of a secret Sith Apprentice trained by [Darth Vader](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Vader "Darth Vader") to hunt the last of the remaining Jedi (effectively using the basis for the *Star Wars: The Force Unleashed* video game). Underneath the secret Sith Apprentice could be a group of Dark Jedi, finally able to be out in the open under the Emperor's reign, or a group of [Clone Troopers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Clone_Troopers "Clone Troopers") trained to fight such a worthy prey. This hunt begins on the trail of [Sarn Vals](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sarn_Vals?action=edit&redlink=1 "Sarn Vals (page does not exist)"), a [Quarren](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Quarren "Quarren") Jedi Knight, whose presence was implicated after a mysterious disturbance in [The Corporate Sector](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Corporate_Sector?action=edit&redlink=1 "The Corporate Sector (page does not exist)").

### [The Rebellion Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rebellion_Era "The Rebellion Era")

Again, this era suffers the restrictions of canon and the Rule of Two. However, a situation similar to the one proposed in [The Dark Times](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Times "The Dark Times") would work just as well. [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi") are all but gone, so a secret Sith Apprentice might lead special strike teams against [The Rebel Alliance](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rebel_Alliance "The Rebel Alliance"). Most likely, such teams will be composed of Dark Jedi or elite [Stormtroopers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Stormtroopers "Stormtroopers"). You still have the option of creating groups in the fringes of the Empire that want nothing to do with the plans of [Darth Sidious](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Sidious "Darth Sidious").

As a specific example, your group might be one of Darth Vader's pet teams, led by his secret apprentice. Since [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi") are no longer a threat, Vader has given the group the most sensitive and distasteful missions in the Empire. Through the capture and interrogation of a [Bothan](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Bothan "Bothan") spy, he has learned that the Rebellion is being aided on the distant planet of [Rentean](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Rentean?action=edit&redlink=1 "Rentean (page does not exist)") in [The Outer Rim](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Outer_Rim?action=edit&redlink=1 "The Outer Rim (page does not exist)"). The [Human](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Human "Human") inhabitants of the planet are secretly building [Starfighters](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Starfighters "Starfighters") for the Alliance. The group is sent in to infiltrate the secret shipbuilding facility, destroy it, and teach the locals what it means to aid the enemies of the Empire- a lesson they won't soon forget.

### [The New Jedi Order Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_New_Jedi_Order_Era "The New Jedi Order Era")

You need clever ideas to create workable Sith adventures for this era, since canon limits it greatly. Any Sith would have to have been hidden for some time or Dark Jedi who stumbled across [Sith Holocrons](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Holocrons "Sith Holocrons") or something similar. Their activities must remain secret; canon shows that the Dark Lady [Lumiya](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lumiya "Lumiya") is the closest thing to Sith encountered in this era by [The New Republic](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_New_Republic "The New Republic"). However, [Lumiya](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lumiya "Lumiya") herself encounters Sith on [Korriban](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Korriban "Korriban") during [The Legacy Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Legacy_Era "The Legacy Era"), and they most certainly would be active during the Yuuzhan Vong Invasion.

As a specific example, your group could consist of several Sith who never subscribed to the Rule of Two but were unable to operate openly due to the enormous power and long reach of [Darth Sidious](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Sidious "Darth Sidious"). Now that he's gone, these Sith grow in power on [The Outer Rim](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Outer_Rim?action=edit&redlink=1 "The Outer Rim (page does not exist)"), recruiting new apprentices from among the Force-sensitive. Stealth and guile still prevail, but under the auspices of building an army that can go out into the galaxy to carve out a piece of [The New Republic](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_New_Republic "The New Republic") for a new Sith Empire.

### [The Legacy Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Legacy_Era "The Legacy Era")

Once again, [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") emerge *en masse* under [Darth Krayt](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Krayt "Darth Krayt"). Groups can occupy any number of roles in the new Empire and can operate openly and with authority. There is resistance to be crushed and old secrets to be unearthed.

As a specific example, your group could repeat history and be Jedi Hunters during [The Sith-Imperial War](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith-Imperial_War "The Sith-Imperial War"). This time, however, instead of being one Sith and a group of Dark Jedi (or troopers), your group consists only of Sith characters. A Jedi conclave has been striking out against the forces of Emperor [Roan Fel](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Roan_Fel "Roan Fel") and [Darth Krayt](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Krayt "Darth Krayt") from a secret hiding place, which your characters have been tasked with finding. Once they have been located, the Jedi are to be wiped out. Of course, things are rarely what they seem- the Jedi are setting a trap.

## Power Beyond Belief: Using Ultra-Powerful Sith Lords in Saga Edition

*By Sterling Hershey*

Amazingly dark and devastating powers are the purview of some of the greatest Sith Lords of [The Old Republic Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Old_Republic_Era "The Old Republic Era"). They cheat death repeatedly. They devastate and consume entire worlds with a thought. They bend and twist [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force") to their needs and desires as [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") warps them into tools of its own. Average beings stand no chance of stopping these ultra-powerful monsters of [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side"). Only the greatest heroes may ultimately defeat them.

In the *Star Wars Roleplaying Game Saga Edition*, using such powerful characters presents many challenges to the Gamemaster and game system itself. This is especially true when balancing story issues with game mechanics. As a GM, how do you handle characters with powers that are far beyond those that the heroes possess?

In the *[Knights of the Old Republic Campaign Guide](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Knights_of_the_Old_Republic_Campaign_Guide "Knights of the Old Republic Campaign Guide")*, the approach taken to adjudicating these powers uses a combination of story devices, [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points"), and existing [Talents](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talents "Talents"), [Feats](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Feats "Feats"), and [Force Powers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Powers "Force Powers"). In this article, we discuss this method. We also provide alternative ideas for GM who wish to apply similar abilities to their own villains by creating specialized mechanics.

Using Powers as Story DevicesUsing Destiny PointsAlternative MechanicsHeroes and Powerful Abilities

The true function of some ultra-powerful abilities is as a story device. Each Sith Lord's power is specific to that character, created through his or her unique circumstances and developed to suit specific plot points. They are unique powers, with no expectation of duplication by other Sith Lords. They are also not powers likely (or able) to be shared or taught to an apprentice.

Using a power as a story device gives GMs maximum flexibility in creating and using their own powerful Sith Lords. The power simply operates as required to fit the plot. However, it is important to realize that while these dark abilities are devastatingly powerful, they are also limited in scope. They generally do not provide a great variety of powers; instead, they dramatically boost a single power or a limited number of related abilities.

Powers used as story devices should be more cinematic in nature and rarely used directly against the heroes. They are best used to shock, frighten, or horrify the heroes and drive the plot forward. If this type of power is used directly against the heroes, be prepared to allow them to make [Skill Checks](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Skill_Checks "Skill Checks"), make attacks, or use other abilities to escape or mitigate its effects. It should not be easy, requiring high [Skill](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Skill "Skill") DCs or even the use of a [Force Point](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Point "Force Point") or [Destiny Point](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Point "Destiny Point").

## Example in Play

[Darth Nihilus](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Nihilus "Darth Nihilus") is literally a destroyer of worlds, powered by an insatiable hunger that drives him to consume greater and greater populations. While one may reasonably expect someone fallen to [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") to employ *[Force Lightning](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Lightning "Force Lightning")* against a foe, no one expects a fleet of [Starships](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Starships "Starships") commanded by Sith Lords consuming entire planets. Nor would Nihilus tolerate such a rival and competitor for his sustenance. Given the Sith Lord's unique background, it is not the type of ability that would be taught to an apprentice.

From a game mechanics point of view, there is little need to spend time developing rules and restrictions for such a unique power. It is a story device. From the GM's perspective, [Darth Nihilus](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Nihilus "Darth Nihilus") consumes a world when the plot requires it. However, lesser aspects of his terrible hunger might be used directly against the heroes. These abilities are better emulated by the use of [Force Talents](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Talents "Force Talents"), [Force Powers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Powers "Force Powers"), [Force Secrets](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Secrets "Force Secrets"), and [Force Techniques](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Techniques "Force Techniques"). In this case, the GM should describe the use of such a Talent, like **[Drain Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Drain_Force "Drain Force")**, in a manner that suggests that it is powered by his unnatural hunger.

A second way to emulate an ultra-powerful ability is through the creative use of [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points"). This encourages creative descriptions that relate to the ability's main use. Since there are a limited number of [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points"), this automatically restricts the number of uses a given power has. This implementation works best with characters that fight against or otherwise interact with the heroes, where tighter integration with familiar mechanics may appeal to a player's sense of fair play.

Using [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points") is a great way to account for powerful abilities in combat. Scoring an automatic [Critical Hit](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Critical_Hit "Critical Hit") could be described as channeling a unique power instead of making a simple blaster shot or [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber") strike. An automatic miss could be described as the absorption of a vast amount of power or deflecting an attack miraculously through [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force"), rather than the simple avoidance of an attack. The ability to act out of turn could emulate a character's amazing quickness on the battlefield or their ability to strike the heroes at an unexpected moment. Even using a [Destiny Point](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Point "Destiny Point") to immediately gain 3 [Force Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Points "Force Points") could be described in a manner that makes a character seem like they are rejuvenating themselves in a mystical fashion, even though they may not use the actual [Force Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Points "Force Points") until later in the encounter. The GM is under no obligation to specify the mechanics behind the effects during the game. However, they should consider doing so after the fact so that the players understand what is happening- even if their characters do not.

Gamemasters may find it useful to combine this option with the story device method. The Sith Lord might gain the regular [Destiny Point](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Point "Destiny Point") benefits or alternative benefits as dictated by the GM. While the story device provides the overall special ability and justifications for the existence of a power, using [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points") allows for limitations on that power. This can be especially useful for new GM characters that the heroes must ultimately defeat. After all, just because an ability is extremely powerful, that doesn't mean its use is unlimited. Over a long adventure or campaign, the heroes might battle an enemy many times, whittling away at their supply of [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points") until it is exhausted- and the enemy is finally defeated.

## Example in Play

[Darth Sion](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Sion "Darth Sion") embodies the unbeatable enemy. He's a Sith so vile that he channels his own anger through [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force") to keep himself alive even though he should be dead. Thanks to his intense anger and pain, he literally holds his decaying body together through [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") of [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force").

Darth Sion's continued existence is best described as a story device, rather than as a specialized [Force Power](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Power "Force Power") that requires constant rolls to keep Sion whole. However, even though the character may believe that his ability to escape death is nearly infinite, it is actually limited by his [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points") in an unusual way. When taking a final hit that would normally kill him, he spends a [Destiny Point](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Point "Destiny Point") to cause the attack to miss, and his story device background returns him to full [Hit Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Hit_Points "Hit Points") and the top of the [Condition Track](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Condition_Track "Condition Track"). [Darth Sion](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Darth_Sion "Darth Sion") cannot be defeated until his [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points") are exhausted. Once they are gone, he feels the loss of power, loses the will to fight, and begins to question his existence. From this point, he is susceptible to The Jedi Exile's attempts to persuade him to give up his life as a much-desired escape from his painful existence.

In their home games, some GMs might wish to use mechanics that emulate a story device or unusual power beyond what regular [Force Powers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Powers "Force Powers"), [Feats](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Feats "Feats"), [Talents](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Talents "Talents"), and class abilities already provide. A GM could create a new [Force Power](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Power "Force Power"), but that implies that the ability could be learned by other Force-users, including the heroes. If that works for your campaign, and the limitations of using it in the [Force Power Suite](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Power_Suite "Force Power Suite") works for your character concept, it is an acceptable option.

However, these ideas usually involve abilities that are beyond the scope of a single [Force Power](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Power "Force Power"). In this case, the Special Quality comes into play. Special Qualities are normally reserved for special class features from [Prestige Classes](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Prestige_Classes "Prestige Classes") or for unusual abilities used by certain [Beasts](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Beasts "Beasts") that are not connected to a [Feat](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Feat "Feat").

In this case, the GM could define the new powerful ability as a Special Quality and write a specific rule that dictates how the power operates every time. This is best used for a power that does not evolve or change over time. It is also expected that this is a narrowly defined mechanic. A power with multiple aspects might require several Special Quality descriptions.

For an example of a Special Quality that covers part of Darth Sion's abilities, here is one based on his similar ability in the *Star Wars* Miniatures Game:

**Eternal Hatred:** Whenever this character would be killed, make a DC 10 [Wisdom](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Wisdom "Wisdom") check; if successful, this character is restored to full [Hit Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Hit_Points "Hit Points") and returned to the top of the [Condition Track](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Condition_Track "Condition Track") instead of being defeated.

Inevitably, some *Knights of the Old Republic* fans and players will want to turn their PCs into ultra-powerful Force-using characters. While the official products assume that the heroes act heroically, some GMs and players may wish to try a campaign or adventure based on [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") or to create a character that requires redemption.

Allowing a player character to gain such powerful abilities poses all of the same difficulties as that of a GM character- without the GM's direct adjudication of the power's use. Therefore, it is best to clearly define the extent of the power's use before play begins. Powers that function as story devices are probably the most difficult to craft and use. In that case, both the GM and the player must agree when the power could come into play. Powers based on [Destiny Points](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny_Points "Destiny Points") would be the most limiting in terms of the amount of use. They are most easily used to enhance [Force Powers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Powers "Force Powers"), [Force Talents](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Force_Talents "Force Talents"), or similar abilities that already exist in the game. Special Qualities are easily the most adaptable option, allowing the GM and player to craft a specific power suited to the campaign.

However, there should be a cost to developing Dark Side abilities. It should be significant and impose a considerable penalty on the character's ability to accomplish other tasks. The severity of the cost and penalty should be proportional to the usefulness and extent of the creative power.

Disfigurement is the most obvious type of cost. Most powerful Sith Lords suffer some kind of major physical deformity or disfigurement. A character who takes on a strong power should suffer a similar fate. It should penalize one or more [Ability Scores](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Ability_Scores "Ability Scores"), including [Charisma](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Charisma "Charisma"). For more on generating the look of a Sith character (whether a player character or a GM character), see Part 2: The Becoming in *Behind the Threat*.

Fortunately, handling ultra-powerful characters is a rare occurrence in *Saga Edition*. Though they are beyond the scope of the average game, they make excellent villains and plot devices. Defeating ultra-powerful characters is also a worthy goal for any long-term campaign. They make effective antagonists for heroes of virtually any level, though only the greatest heroes may ultimately overcome them.

## Mythmaking in *Star Wars*

*By Matthew Grau*

Over the last fifty years, more and more writers have begun to deliberately use what Joseph Campbell coined the monomyth. Through extensive study, Campbell came to believe that nearly all myths, even if from radically different times or places, kept to the same basic structure with the same basic stages. In essence, the monomyth speaks to all people in all places in all times because it represents a commonality of human experience and understanding. It is a powerful storytelling tool when used properly.

George Lucas made deliberate use of the monomyth when creating *Star Wars*, which is part of what has made these space opera stories become modern myths that have endured now through several generations. In fact, the monomyth and its elements have become so central to the *Star Wars* franchise that if it were missing, whatever it was missing from would not be *Star Wars*. These sections will explore the monomyth and its elements as they apply to *Star Wars* and teach you how to use them in your *Star Wars* roleplaying games. Adopting them as practice will not only make your games more epic, they will take on an undeniable character that can only be part of the modern myth that is the *Star Wars* franchise.

In the first section, we will explore the nature of the Hero and then go on to look at other character archetypes in the following two. In sections four and five, we will explore the various stages of the hero's journey. At each step along the way, we will show how these concepts were used in different pieces of the *Star Wars* franchise.

The HeroArchetypes, Part IArchetypes, Part IIMonomyth Structure, Part IMonomyth Structure, Part II

Hero. The word conjures many images in our minds the moment we hear it. For some, it is the red-caped Superman, racing to save the planet from certain destruction. For others, it is the two-fisted adventurer exploring places unknown. For some, it is the soldier bravely serving his country in a foreign land. And for others, it is the fireman risking his life to save people from the burning rubble of the Twin Towers. The word itself is Greek, meaning "To protect and serve." In nearly any example we can think of, this is what our heroes do. They are the ones that save us when no one else can. They are the ones that put their lives on the line for the good of those around them.

## What Makes a Hero

What we discover in the monomyth is that, regardless of culture, all of our heroes have things in common. This is absolutely true in *Star Wars*. The Exile, Obi-Wan Kenobi, Galen Marek, Luke Skywalker, Han Solo, Cade Skywalker, and a host of other heroes from the many eras of *Star Wars* all fit the following criteria which make them fit the label hero.

Most apparently, heroes do things. They do not accomplish great things by sitting around and talking about or contemplating things. Certainly, thought and counsel are important factors in making good decisions, particularly when sizable or difficult consequences hang in the balance, but action is what is required of the hero in order to overcome the things that face him. Obi-Wan does not sit in his hut on the edge of the Dune Sea contemplating what Leia's message might mean to the galaxy. Anakin Skywalker does not wonder what General Grievous will do with the captured Chancellor Palpatine. They spring into action, because that is what is needed.

Heroes live on the razor's edge. To them, death is not some faceless thing that is years yet coming. They face death on a regular basis, defying it in unbelievable ways. To the hero, each moment is precious and nothing can be taken for granted. Each day may very be the last. This may not be the case at every moment during the hero's life, but it is the case in all the points that make the epic stories of which they are a part.

### A Hero's Values

As our (the audience's) window into the story, there is always something with which we can identify about our heroes. There are things about them that are universal, whether it be their personal traits or history, or their motivations. We can see something of ourselves in them. Luke Skywalker begins his journey as a young farmer who seeks something grander than what he has and, as an adoptee, some answers about the family he never knew. Starkiller, Vader's secret apprentice, struggles with his sense of self- should he follow the ways of his corrupt "father" or should he follow his heart as he is drawn to the light. Obi-Wan Kenobi is a patriot, fighting for a way of life in which he deeply believes. Life and family, personal identity, conviction- these are but a few examples, but ones with which we, regardless of culture or age, can identify. Through that identification, we can experience the story more fully for we understand it more fully, regardless of trappings.

Furthermore, no hero is perfect. As there are traits or history or motivations that are universal, each hero also has some kind of understandable flaw. After all, we are all flawed and it allows us to further identify with our heroes if they suffer the same human frailties as the rest of us. As they say, we might like or even lust after perfect people, but we fall in love with those who are flawed- not in spite of the flaws, but because of them. Luke Skywalker is brash and impatient, like many other young men his age. Starkiller is, fundamentally, the product of an abusive household and his ideas of how the world works are simply off. Obi-Wan Kenobi is an idealist and an optimist, sometimes blinded to the corruption and treachery right under his nose. We all suffer from being young at one point, many have had to deal with the tragedy of abuse, and most people want to see the best in others. Their flaws are ours.

### Unique Heroes

Despite their universal characteristics, heroes are always separate. There is something in who they are, perhaps their destiny, that sets them apart from those around them. Han Solo is a smuggler, but also a former Imperial officer who fled the Empire after being exposed to its corruption. He is a criminal, but one with a heart of gold, and this makes him different from the blood-thirsty scoundrels that are a part of his world. Cade Skywalker never fully fit into a life of bounty hunting and piracy, as he was a former Jedi and heir to the Skywalker legacy. His predecessor Luke grew up on Tatooine, a desert planet on the Outer Rim, dreaming of becoming a pilot and leaving the provincial world his fellows found comfortable. Little does he know he is set apart further as the only son of Darth Vader. In every case, the destiny that draws heroes to great things does not let them simply fit in with the world.

Sacrifice is essential to heroism and there is always some element of it in the hero's journey. Heroes must give of something of themselves, something of which they love, or simply suffer mind, body, and soul in order to overcome the great odds that face them. Their journeys are not those of everyday mortals and as such the things they have at risk are so much greater. Princess Leia suffers the loss of her homeworld [Alderaan](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Alderaan "Alderaan") and the cold-blooded murder of every man, woman, and child on that peaceful planet. Starkiller must give his life in battle with the Emperor in order to save the existence of the nascent Rebel Alliance. Luke Skywalker loses his aunt, uncle, and mentor to the Empire, nearly loses his friends, and loses a hand in order to finally bring down Darth Vader and Emperor Palpatine. When the game is great, so are the stakes and sometimes the heroes lose. However, triumph is nothing without setbacks and joy is nothing without tragedy.

## A Hero's Journey

Through their journey, heroes grow. They are never the same after their adventure. These changes are not simply cosmetic either, though such changes may be a part. They are fundamental, the kind of things that change the very core of who the heroes are. Luke Skywalker transforms from a whiny kid from a backwater planet with big dreams into a full-blown Jedi Knight who resurrects a nearly extinct tradition. Starkiller grows from his roots as a child raised in the ways of [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith") to turn his back on his ways and become a Jedi and an integral part in the formation of [The Rebel Alliance](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rebel_Alliance "The Rebel Alliance"). Cade Skywalker transforms from a young Jedi and witness to terrible tragedy to a self-serving bounty hunter back into a Jedi with powerful healing abilities. In the *Star Wars* roleplaying game, players have another measure of growth- Character Level. As heroes in the roleplaying game grow, their experience is translated literally into game statistics. However, advancement in game statistics is something that should only be used as a tool to the greater story and not an end unto itself. They can easily be used to facilitate the characters' growth as heroes as part of their epic journeys.

Though obviously called by [Destiny](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny "Destiny"), heroes may be willing or unwilling to answer. All heroes, as we'll explore in the third article in this series, have a moment of pause before they launch on their adventure and this is different from being unwilling. For example, Luke Skywalker has a moment on [Tatooine](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Tatooine "Tatooine") where he questions whether leaving his family to follow Obi-Wan is something he should do. Ultimately, after his family is killed by Stormtroopers, he willingly leaves. On the other hand, Han Solo was dragged into Luke and Leia's story against his will. He was firmly committed to his life of crime and simply picked up Luke and Obi-Wan because he needed to pay off Jabba the Hutt. Next thing he knows, his ship is being pulled into [The Death Star](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Death_Star "The Death Star") and the rest is history.

Some heroes don't immediately appear to be what most would consider to be heroes on the surface. They are anti-heroes, characters that perform heroic actions while not being heroic themselves. Again, Han Solo is an example of this. He braves death to rescue Princess Leia aboard [The Death Star](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Death_Star "The Death Star"), going above and beyond the call of the simple mercenary, even though it appears his only motivation is financial gain. Cade Skywalker might be another example, who is drawn to heroics despite having embraced a life of bounty hunting and piracy. On the flip side, Starkiller would not be an example of an anti-hero. He begins his story arc as a villain, who undergoes a change of heart as he is exposed to the greater universe. When he becomes a hero, he becomes a hero.

### Heroes of Myth

While in many myths of the West the heroes are loners (or close to it), heroes of the East most often come in groups. *Star Wars* more closely follows the myths of the East in the this regard- heroes always come in groups. In [The Old Republic Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Old_Republic_Era "The Old Republic Era"), the reformed Revan travelled with Carth Onasi, Bastila Shan, Mission Vao, and others. During [The Rise of the Empire Era](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Rise_of_the_Empire_Era "The Rise of the Empire Era"), Obi-Wan Kenobi and Anakin Skywalker fought with Padmé Amidala, Ahsoka Tano, R2-D2, Commander Cody, Captain Rex, and others. During the Rebellion, Luke Skywalker fought with Leia Organa, Han Solo, Chewbacca, R2-D2, C-3PO, and more. Even Starkiller, though supposedly a lone Sith apprentice, had his people- the droid PROXY, Juno Eclipse, and eventually Rahm Kota. This norm works perfectly with the *Star Wars* roleplaying game, where players gather with groups of characters, all who are intended to be the heroes.

Keeping all this in mind, at the center of your story are your heroes- the characters your players have created. The *Star Wars* roleplaying game provides you with all the mechanics to create heroes of all kinds. This analysis of heroes should give you a better base from which to build the non-mechanical aspects of your characters, so that they are truly befitting the mantle of *Star Wars*.

Famous psychologist Carl Jung posited that basic themes of life emerge through consistent patterns in the collective unconscious. He referred to them as archetypes. For what we are discussing here, that simply means that archetypes are types of characters that have significant impact in any story because they speak to common types of people and human experience, regardless of geography or culture. In that context, they fit perfectly within the idea of the monomyth.

Archetypal characters become tools that fulfill useful functions in your story, that help it also fit into the eternal resonance that is the monomyth. Once you master these concepts, you will be able to populate your *Star Wars* game with such archetypes and they will help you in your task as Gamemaster. You will come to understand their importance as you read on.

Here, we are going to look at three key archetypes as they apply to *Star Wars*: the Mentor, the Threshold Guardian, and the Herald.

## The Mentor

Originally referred to as the Wise Old Man/Woman by Joseph Campbell, this archetype is perhaps more easily known as the Mentor. The characters that teach, guide, or protect the Heroes or provide them with important gifts are Mentors. They are designed to help prepare the Hero for the trials ahead, or to guide the Hero through them. Though they may arrive later, Mentors usually appear early in the story when the Hero needs the most guidance. They also motivate the Hero to action and help him overcome his fears. One example of this is the relationship between Ben Kenobi and Luke Skywalker. Young Luke meets Ben just as his destiny begins to unfold. If it were not for Ben, Luke may have given into his fears and stayed on [Tatooine](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Tatooine "Tatooine")- even after the death of his Aunt and Uncle. Ben takes on the role of Mentor in many ways. He tells Luke about his father Anakin, gives the boy his first [Lightsaber](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Lightsaber "Lightsaber"), and begins to tutor him in the ways of [The Jedi](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi "The Jedi"). Without Ben's influence, Luke would never have become a Jedi, may never have left [Tatooine](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Tatooine "Tatooine"), and would have most likely been killed by [Stormtroopers](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Stormtroopers "Stormtroopers") looking for C-3PO and R2-D2. Luke owed a great deal of who he was and who he became to his Mentor.

Sometimes, as an aspect of their instruction, Mentors must act as the Hero's conscience as they waver in their own judgment or wrestles with their own emotions. The relationship between Obi-Wan Kenobi and Anakin Skywalker is an excellent example of this. Anakin's impulsive nature and deep personal anger consistently move him to make poor decisions, or at least not decisions befitting a Jedi, during his years as an apprentice. Obi-Wan was always there to correct his action or thinking, to guide him in the ways of [The Jedi Code](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi_Code "The Jedi Code") and teach him to be mindful of [The Living Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Living_Force "The Living Force"). Even after Anakin became a Jedi Knight, he still looked to his master for guidance, on some level knowing that his own internal moral compass did not point true north.

There are those Heroes who are already on their journey, whose Mentor comes from the inside- oftentimes in the manifestation of a code. In *Star Wars*, both [The Jedi Code](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi_Code "The Jedi Code") and [The Sith Code](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith_Code?action=edit&redlink=1 "The Sith Code (page does not exist)") immediately spring to mind. Once Darth Malak betrays Revan, he becomes the Dark Lord, beholden to none. What guides him in his conquest is the Code of the Sith, which preaches passion, strength, and power. Obi-Wan Kenobi, a Mentor to others, draws his strength from [The Jedi Code](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Jedi_Code "The Jedi Code"), which practices control, inner peace, and harmony. Whenever these types of characters need guidance, they can look to their own internal mentor to help them find their way.

Mentors don't have to be put together Heroes in their own right. It is perfectly okay for them to be damaged or broken in some kind of way. Jolee Bindo acts, in his own way, as a Mentor to the amnesiac Revan, though he is a rebellious Jedi, occasionally blathering, and somewhere between Light and Dark in his adherence to [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force"). Both of Luke Skywalker's Mentors, Ben Kenobi and Yoda, have been utterly defeated and outcast. Yet, in these cases and others, the Mentor's contribution to the Hero was still profound.

Following that line of thinking, Mentors can also be dark in nature. In *Star Wars*, many of these kinds of mentors are villains in their own right. Chancellor Palpatine, before his disfigurement, was such a Mentor to Anakin Skywalker. Not outright evil, at least not on the surface, the Chancellor had been a Mentor to the young Jedi since Anakin was a boy and his guidance was most certainly dark at times. Ultimately, it was designed to mentor the young Jedi down the path of [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side"). Then you have have relationships such as that of Darth Vader and Starkiller, wherein Vader's tutelage was that of a Sith Lord and dark and cruel as it comes.

Many times the Mentor also functions as a parental figure. Ben Kenobi became a father-figure to Luke Skywalker, Qui-Gon Jinn the same to a young Obi-Wan Kenobi, and Chancellor Palpatine the same to Anakin Skywalker. These are but a few examples, though this type of Mentor-Hero relationship runs through *Star Wars*.

## The Threshold Guardian

Another archetype pertinent to the monomyth is the Threshold Guardian. Essentially, while the Heroes travel on their paths, there are characters that will arise to prevent them from easily stepping across the threshold into whatever new world is next. They are not the primary villains, but rather are designed to test the Hero, whether it be their abilities or their resolve, to make sure that they are ready for the challenges that lay before them. They prevent the Hero from charging ahead without being fully prepared.

These characters manifest in many ways in *Star Wars*. On the benign end, Owen Lars was a Threshold Guardian to young Luke Skywalker. Owen was firmly against Luke leaving the farm, for reasons of his nephew's safety. He was a living test to see if Luke was willing to embrace his [Destiny](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny "Destiny"), even though it was not in his upbringing or convenient for him to do so. The Jedi Council acts as a Threshold Guardian to Anakin Skywalker. First, the Council tries to keep the young boy from training, then they keep him as an apprentice for a good long time, then they refuse to confer on him the title of Jedi Master, and then they try to keep him from a seat on the Council itself. They test him at many points along the way. Imagine how swiftly and dangerously Anakin would have fallen to [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") if it weren't for the Council's vigilance.

On the other hand, there are more violent manifestations of this archetype. Darth Maul is a Threshold Guardian to Obi-Wan Kenobi. Only if Obi-Wan can defeat the Sith hammer can he go on to deal with what waits for him in the Clone Wars. Darth Tyrannus tests Anakin Skywalker to see if he is indeed ready to walk down the path of being the Chancellor's apprentice. Calo Nord opposes Revan to see if they are ready to take their place as a Hero of the Jedi. The Droid PROXY continuously tests Starkiller to make certain that he is worthy of the title Sith. Eventually, the secret apprentice must destroy the Droid to cross the threshold into his rejection of [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side").

## The Herald

Where Threshold Guardians test the Hero, characters that are manifestations of the Herald archetype come to shake up the Hero's world and get them on their path. The appearance of a Herald is the catalyst that makes it so that the Hero can no longer live in the ordinary world they have called home. In essence, they are the energy that motivates the Hero to be a Hero.

Every Hero in *Star Wars* has his or her own personal Herald. Young Anakin Skywalker would have been content to remain Watto's slave if not for Qui-Gon Jinn entering his life. The older Jedi discovers the boy's natural power in [The Force](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Force "The Force") and brings him out into the greater universe. Luke Skywalker begins on his path not because of the Droids, but because of a message by Princess Leia Organa. Compelled by her plight, he goes off to meet his Mentor and ultimately realize his [Destiny](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Destiny "Destiny"). Bastila Shan is a Herald for the amnesiac Revan, the bond they share drawing the former Sith Lord on a path that will crush [The Sith Empire](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith_Empire "The Sith Empire"). Starkiller's Herald might not be so obvious- Juno Eclipse. It is her appearance in his life that sparks the first feelings that blossom into love, one of the primary reasons the secret apprentice starts to question his path with [The Sith](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith "The Sith").

To review, famous psychologist Carl Jung posited that basic themes of life emerge through consistent patterns in the collective unconscious. He referred to them as archetypes. For what we are discussing here, that simply means that archetypes are types of characters that have significant impact in any story because they speak to common types of people and human experience, regardless of geography or culture. In that context, they fit perfectly within the idea of the monomyth.

In Archetypes, Part I, we looked at the Mentor, the Threshold Guardian, and the Herald. Here, we are going to look at three more key archetypes as they apply to *Star Wars*: the Shadow, the Shapeshifter, and the Trickster. When we're done, we're going to explore how to utilize all of the archetypes in your *Star Wars* adventures.

## The Shadow

The Shadow archetype, as in psychology, represents the dark side of things. The Shadow is made up of all the things the Hero doesn't like about themselves or the world. Shadows typify the demons that whisper to the Hero, the skeletons in his closet. Most times, the Shadow is represented by the villain or villains, who are often the mirror into which the Hero must look and also overcome in themselves what lurks beneath. Shadows are not always vile and evil- they often have humanizing qualities (or qualities with which one can identify, in the case of aliens).

The Shadows in *Star Wars* are so often Sith. Luke Skywalker fought against his Shadow in Darth Vader, who represented the brutality and oppression against which the young Hero fought. However, Vader also wanted to be with the son who had been taken from him- a normal and very human emotion. He was also an example to Luke of what the young Jedi could become if he gave in to his anger and hate. Revan's Shadow was Darth Malak, ruler of [The Sith Empire](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Sith_Empire "The Sith Empire") and scourge of the galaxy. There was little in Malak that one could call humanizing, but his traits of greed and envy are common to the human condition. Most importantly, he was the mirror to Revan, a reminder of what they once were and could easily become again.

Shadows may also be seductive. Anakin Skywalker fought against his Shadow in Chancellor Palpatine. Unknown to Anakin, Palpatine was a [Sith Lord](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/Sith_Lord "Sith Lord") that embraced everything that the Jedi rejected and was bringing democracy to its knees. However, Palpatine also treated the boy like a son (or at least a nephew) for more than a decade, protecting and guiding him. In the end, Palpatine used Anakin's fears and desires against him, luring him to [The Dark Side](https://swse.miraheze.org/wiki/The_Dark_Side "The Dark Side") with promises of power over life and death. Stories and Heroes are, in many ways, defined by their villains. The better the villain, the better the story. It is important to create a powerful Shadow antagonist if one wishes to resonate with the monomyth.

## The Shapeshifter

Always seeming to be changing are the Shapeshifters. They are never consistent for very long. Their chaotic natures are designed to bring about further suspense in the story, as well as to create doubt for the hero. Oftentimes, the role of the Shapeshifter is taken on by characters for stretches of the story, to fulfill the aforementioned dramatic purpose. Anakin Skywalker, even as an adult, had traits of a Shapeshifter. One never knew which Anakin he would get- the noble Jedi, the angry boy, the loving husband, the rebellious apprentice. Sometimes in conversation, he would cycle through several of these manifestations. Princess Leia Organa, with regards to her relationship with Han Solo, was a Shapeshifter. Was she going to thank him or pick a fight? Was she going to insult him or kiss him? Their relationship was rocky and uncertain, causing dramatic tension between the characters- until they finally fall in love and things stabilize.

Characters that typify the outsider are also often Shapeshifters. The outsider is a character than comes from an unusual, sometimes tragic, background, who remains aloof from the other characters. They dispense wisdom, but are also not necessarily to be relied upon. Han Solo is a perfect example. He is a former Imperial officer turned outlaw smuggler, who pretends to be mean and self-serving while hiding his heart of gold. At varying times, he is mercenary, kind, arrogant, caring, and more. Until his capture and incarceration by Boba Fett, Han Solo is a moving target in terms of his personality.

## The Trickster
